Of the Vanitie and Uncertaintie of Artes and Sciences (Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa (trans. James Sandford), 1569)
==========================================================
Sandford's 1569 English translation of Agrippa's De Vanitate; the great skeptical attack on all arts and sciences. Public domain. Digitized from Internet Archive (https://archive.org/details/bim_early-english-books-1475-1640_henrie-cornelius-of-_agrippa-h-c_1569).

To the Noble and Vertuous Prince
Thomas Duke of Northfolke,


Earle Marfhal of England, one of the


Queenes eMaiefties mofte Honorable Priuie
Councel, and Knight of the Honorable Order
of the Garter, CHC, |


Eneas the Lapitha
( renowmed Prince) he that
fought with the Centaures, was
fo harde to be wouded, and with
weapons to be pearced (as Pin-
darus Writeth ) that when the
| Centaures with one conſent aſ—
failinge him, had layde vppon
hima huge heape of woodes, he
not wit hſtandinge was not van-
g uiſhe d.. But this our Agrippa, not weapons of Vul-
cane, as Achilles and Eneas did, but of reafon , whiche not
onely matntaineth and defendeth the truthe,but alfè with deape
inſighte trieth , and as it were, fifteth the fame from the falfe .
So that the truth, by realons meane; is made pure and inuiolate,
and the untruth whiche obſcuret h it, is plainely perceaued. This
man, madle not {harpe warre with the Centaures, as the ot her
did, but well neare with all Eſtates, Artes, and Sciences, whofe
abuſes, he hath fo vanquiſhed and put to flight, that mofte menne
will afigne him the palme and price of Victorie How many haue
deſired knowledges, Artes, and Sciences, and how fiwe haue dete-
éted their deceitful abufes? Many feeke the good, but few find out
the enill that lieth hidden therein : it is no leſſe prayfe to embrace
vertue, then diſpraiſe not to {hun vice, the rewarde of vertue is
euerlaſting fame and the guerdon of vice, is {hamefull infamie:he
is Wife that is not deceaued by the counterfait [hew of vertue,and
he mofte unwife,that rafhly chuſeth that whiche femeth good. for


vices oftentimes put on the coloure of vertue. Mironides a lear-

Epiſtle Dedicatorie.


ned Philoſopher, and a wife Capitaine: ſaide, I hat the wiſedome
of a man, is not ſomuc he knowen by withdrawinge himfelfe from
the euill as by chooſinge the good, becaufe that commonly under
the euill any good cannot be. hidden, but under the good muche


euill maye be diſſmbled. Enen as the Enfeigne beginneth Per


fignum Crucis, and endeth in Satanas and Barrabas: So like-
wife the great euils take their beginninge in fime good deedes, in
ſuche wife that they be counterfaited like Maſ kes, fugred like
Pilles, and gilted lke Reubarbe. There is no ma ſe fenfeles which
keapeth not himſelſe from the euill, whiche is notoriouſiy enill, but
a wife man will beware of that whiche is knowen not to be alto-
geather good. Themiftocles the Philoſipher, put all his felicitie
in deſcending from a noble linage. Simonides the Philofepher,
accompted the greateſt happineſſe to be well beloued of the peo-
pie. Antiſthenes put all his felicitie in renomme after his death.
‘Bat Herillus placed the ſoueraigne good in learninge and know-
ledge, who ſhooteth wide of the marke for that, in no Wife can
be good, whiche is vaine, and uncertaine : howe than can the
chiefeft felicitie he founde therein ? F neede not malę in this
place, any longe diſcourſe to diſproue the vanitee of Artes and


Sciences, ſithe this Authour hath written thereof at large whiche


Ingliſbed, ? addreſſe to your Grace , moſte humbly befeechinge

vou, to accepte it as a pledge of aminde well affected towardes
‘your Honoure, whiche haue not onely the felicitie of Themiſto-
cles, to wete, honoure of Parentage, but which is more, haue alſi
confirmed the fame With Honour purchafed by your owne ver-
tue: yon haue the felicitie of Simonides , for you are well beloued
A the people : and alſo dyinge ſnall haue the ſoueraigne good of
Antiſthenes, for your fame {hall forener ſounde in the eares of
men: but to you lininge,} wijhe Neſtors yeares, with continuall
health aud encreafe of Honour.


Jour Graces, moft humblie
to commaunde,


Games Sanford,

To the Reader.
„in althinges (gentle Rea-


Ja Pts der) with which God endowed man at his


n creation nothinge is more to be eſteemed


Realon, it foloweth,that the fruites


thereof,that is, learningeand knowledge
ee fhoulde be had in price. For what is more
to be defired then knowledge, thé learning, then wifdome,
whiche teacheth man to afpire to heauenly thinges, which
guideth his doinges, and ruleth his affaires: wherefore
‘PhocilidesTaithe very well, Viſdome gonerneth Fieldes,Citses, P
and Shippes. For al thinges be happely exployted,which b
the helpe of wiſdome are gouerned. Sapience proceedet
of perfecte Reaſon, ioygned with Learninge, and Knowe-
ledge, whiche if it be true, then N it foloweth,
that Artes and Sciences are good. And although this Au-
thoure ſharply inueigheth againſt them (which to the rade
multitude for that cauſe, maye feme naught and noyfome)
et his intent is, not to deface the worthinefle of Artes and
de ences, but to reproue and detecte theire euil vſes, and de-
clare the excellencie of his wit in diſprouinge them, for a
fhewe of Learning: which euil vfes,doubtles haue creptin,
thorough the peruerfe doings of men. There is nothing in
this world, whicl1 is not corrupted,nor any learning which
is not abuſed, notwithſtãding the whole circle or compaſſe
of Learning(the Seuẽ Liberall Sciences I meane, called £z-
clopædia is not to be neglected, and all humane Artes ro-
ſecded: for like as difeafed bodies, hauinge fome infirmitie,
are not to be abandoned, but to be recured, preferued, and
cheariſned, ſo ought Sciẽces and Knowledges(though there
Jurke in them fome abuſe) not to be dif pise » but with all
endeuour ought to be purged frõ their chaffe,aud brought
to their former perfection. V Vhowoulde geuecredence
to this Authoure, and allow him, if he endeuoured to diſa-
low al learninges? it is a vaine thing to diſpraiſe all Know-
ledges, anda praiſe to detecte their vanitie. The Authour

whiche haft deſtroyed me. So foorthwith the dogge depar-

dogge was without doubt a Diuel of Hell. The naughtines

he time caufed him to be naught,and to perfeuer in er-
roure, for if he fhoulde altogeather haue reuolted and gone

enormities, in part alfo he renounceth his Canons, as in the

like,defending alfo the Royal prefence of Chrift his body.
It is likely, that if he had ben in as good a time as this is, he

ut to euill, is cauſe of mutuall deſtruction: Epiétetus the

toicke Philofopher ſaithe: Vnderſtande, if thy companiõ

whiche I haue put into my tranflation , the places where
this is added, are in the 96.98. 100.101. Cap. betweene twoo
ſtarres with this forme x placed in the Margent. I conie-
cture that this booke in the 7talian was tranflated out of
that Copie,whiche the Authoure firft publifhed,and fince
that time other bookes of the fame fort haue ben printed,
and in printing fome thinges haue ben either depraued by

tnueigh againſte the Popes folowers , whiche coniecture is


likelieft,for negligence of the Printer woulde haue appea-


red afwell in other places, as in thefe, if he beinge rechlefle,
had don things without aduiſement. V Vherfore(friendly
Reader) wel conſtrue my doinges,take in good part iny la-
bours,amende the faultes that are ouerpafled in printinge,
and defende this againſte the malitious detractions of en-
uious Zoilus, then ſhall I accompte thee my friende, thinke


my time well ſpente, and my paines requited. p
